- For each `i`, switch on `indx[i]` (1 to 4), jump to a label, compute `a[i] += square(some array element)`, then go to `L50`.  
- If `indx[i] == 4`, it falls through to `L50` after the addition.  

Important: `a[i]` is updated **once per inner loop iteration** based on `indx[i]`, using one of `b[i]`, `c[i]`, `d[i]`, `e[i]`.  
The switch uses computed goto-style jumps, but it’s just a way to pick which array to square and add to `a[i]`.  

Potential semantic mismatch in optimized code (common mistakes):  
- If optimized code tries to vectorize by grouping cases, it must ensure each `i` still uses its own `indx[i]` to pick the source array.  
- If optimized code reorders operations across `i` (like doing all case 1 first), but `a[i]` is only updated once per iteration, that’s fine if `a[i]` is not reused across cases incorrectly.  
- But if `a[i]` is updated multiple times per inner loop (once per case group), that changes semantics because in source code only one update happens per `i` per inner loop.  

Let’s test with a small example:  

**Example 1**:  
`LEN_1D = 3`, `iterations = 2` (so `nl` loops once).  
`indx = [1, 2, 3]`  
`a = [0,0,0]`, `b=[1,1,1]`, `c=[2,2,2]`, `d=[3,3,3]`, `e=[4,4,4]`.  

Source execution:  
`nl=0`:  
- `i=0`, case 1: `a[0] += 1*1` → `a[0]=1`  
- `i=1`, case 2: `a[1] += 2*2` → `a[1]=4`  
- `i=2`, case 3: `a[2] += 3*3` → `a[2]=9`  

Final `a = [1,4,9]`.  

If optimized code groups by case but still updates each `a[i]` only once, same result. If it does something like:  
```
for i in case1_indices:
    a[i] += b[i]*b[i]
for i in case2_indices:
    a[i] += c[i]*c[i]
...
```
That’s fine because each `i` appears in exactly one case list per `nl` iteration.  

But if `indx[i]` changes between `nl` iterations? No, `indx` is constant.  

So grouping by case is semantically correct.  

But the unit test says semantics inconsistent — maybe the optimized code incorrectly updates `a[i]` for multiple cases when `indx[i]` matches multiple after some transformation?  

Let’s check a tricky case:  

**Example 2**:

`i=1`: case 4 → `a[1] += 2*2 = 4`  
`i=2`: case 4 → `a[2] += 3*3 = 9`

then all `i` with `indx==2`:

Same result.  

So grouping by `indx` is safe.

Compiler Feedback:


UnitTest Feedback:

Unit Test analysis: 
Source code and optimized code semantics are inconsistent.


**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

void s442_opt(int iterations,int LEN_1D, float* a, float *b,float* c, float *d, float* e, int* indx)

    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations/2; nl++) {
        // Split loop by case to remove branching and enable vectorization
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            if (indx[i] == 1) {
                a[i] += b[i] * b[i];
            }

}

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
Let’s go step by step.  

---

**1. Compilation check**  
The optimized code compiles without errors — no syntax issues.

**2. Semantic comparison**  

The original code processes each `i` exactly once per outer loop iteration, applying **exactly one** of the four possible updates to `a[i]` based on `indx[i]`.  

The optimized code splits into four separate loops, each scanning all `i` again.  
This changes the semantics because:  

- In the original, if `indx[i] == 1`, only `b[i]*b[i]` is added to `a[i]` in that iteration of `nl`.

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

## Analysis

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code
The optimized code contains a **compilation error**: it declares variable-length arrays (VLAs) with size `LEN_1D` on the stack:
```c
int idx1[LEN_1D], idx2[LEN_1D], idx3[LEN_1D], idx4[LEN_1D];
```
If `LEN_1D` is large (e.g., 1,000,000), this will cause a stack overflow. Additionally, VLAs are not standard C++ (though they are C99). This is a critical error that prevents safe execution.

**Fix**: Replace VLAs with dynamic allocation or separate index arrays sized by counting occurrences first.

Since there is a compilation/execution safety issue, we **stop here** and output **FAIL**.

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

void s442_opt(int iterations,int LEN_1D, float* a, float *b,float* c, float *d, float* e, int* indx)
{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations/2; nl++) {
        // First pass: count occurrences of each case
        int cnt1 = 0, cnt2 = 0, cnt3 = 0, cnt4 = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            int idx = indx[i];
            cnt1 += (idx == 1);
            cnt2 += (idx == 2);
            cnt3 += (idx == 3);
            cnt4 += (idx == 4);
        }
        
        // Allocate index arrays
        int* idx1 = (int*)malloc(cnt1 * sizeof(int));
        int* idx2 = (int*)malloc(cnt2 * sizeof(int));
        int* idx3 = (int*)malloc(cnt3 * sizeof(int));
        int* idx4 = (int*)malloc(cnt4 * sizeof(int));
        
        // Second pass: fill index arrays
        int pos1 = 0, pos2 = 0, pos3 = 0, pos4 = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            int idx = indx[i];
            if (idx == 1) idx1[pos1++] = i;
            else if (idx == 2) idx2[pos2++] = i;
            else if (idx == 3) idx3[pos3++] = i;
            else if (idx == 4) idx4[pos4++] = i;
        }
        
        // Process each case in separate vectorizable loops
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt1; j++) {
            int i = idx1[j];
            a[i] += b[i] * b[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt2; j++) {
            int i = idx2[j];
            a[i] += c[i] * c[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt3; j++) {
            int i = idx3[j];
            a[i] += d[i] * d[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt4; j++) {
            int i = idx4[j];
            a[i] += e[i] * e[i];
        }
        
        // Free allocated memory
        free(idx1);
        free(idx2);
        free(idx3);
        free(idx4);
    }
}

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
## Analysis  

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code  
The optimized code compiles without errors.  

### 2. Semantic Comparison  
The original code processes each element in a single loop with a switch and computed goto (or branch) per element.  
The optimized code:  
- Counts occurrences of each case in the first pass.  
- Allocates arrays to store indices for each case.  
- Fills those arrays in a second pass.  
- Processes each case in separate loops.  

This changes the **order of updates** to `a[i]`. In the original code, updates happen in the original index order `i = 0..LEN_1D-1`.  
In the optimized code, all `indx[i]==1` updates happen first, then all `indx[i]==2`, etc.  

Since floating-point addition is **not associative** (due to rounding), reordering can change final results.  
Also, if the same `i` appears multiple times across iterations (not the case here because `indx` is fixed per iteration, but `a[i]` accumulates across `nl` loops), the order still matters because in the original, all updates for `i` happen before moving to `i+1` in the same `nl` iteration.  

In the optimized version, within one `nl` iteration, all `i` with case 1 are updated first, then case 2, etc. This is **semantically different** from the original when considering floating-point non-associativity.  

**Example check**:  
Let `LEN_1D=2`, `a[0]=0, a[1]=0`, `b[0]=1, b[1]=2`, `c[0]=3, c[1]=4`, `indx[0]=1, indx[1]=2`.  
Original:  
- `i=0`: `a[0] += b[0]*b[0]` → `a[0]=1`  
- `i=1`: `a[1] += c[1]*c[1]` → `a[1]=16`  

Optimized:  
- Case 1 loop: `a[0] += b[0]*b[0]` → `a[0]=1`  
- Case 2 loop: `a[1] += c[1]*c[1]` → `a[1]=16`  

Results match here, but if `indx[0]=2, indx[1]=1`:  
Original:  
- `i=0`: `a[0] += c[0]*c[0]` → `a[0]=9`  
- `i=1`: `a[1] += b[1]*b[1]` → `a[1]=4`  

Optimized:  
- Case 1 loop: `a[1] += b[1]*b[1]` → `a[1]=4`  
- Case 2 loop: `a[0] += c[0]*c[0]` → `a[0]=9`  

Same final values, but order of operations changed. For floating-point, if `a[0]` and `a[1]` were same variable (they’re not), order matters. Here, they are independent per index, so reordering across different `i` is safe because each `a[i]` is only updated once per `nl` iteration.  

But wait — in the original, each `i` is processed exactly once per `nl` iteration, and `indx[i]` determines which array’s square is added. In the optimized version, each `i` is also processed exactly once per `nl` iteration, just grouped by case. Since each `a[i]` is independent, grouping by case doesn’t change final `a[i]` values.  

Thus, **semantics are preserved** for independent per-element updates.  

Unit test passes → confirms semantics match.  

### 3. Vectorization Analysis  
Compiler feedback shows:  
- First counting loop is vectorized.  
- Second filling loop is not vectorized due to switch-like branching and storing to different arrays.  
- The four final loops are not vectorized because compiler cannot determine array bounds (`a[i]`, `b[i]`, etc., where `i` is from index arrays).  

The main issue: indirect indexing (`a[idx1[j]]`) prevents vectorization because compiler cannot prove no aliasing and cannot gather efficiently without guarantees.  

**Optimization opportunity**:  
The current optimized code introduces overhead (malloc/free per outer iteration) and prevents vectorization in the compute loops.  
Better approach:  
- Remove dynamic allocation inside the loop.  
- Use separate arrays for each case’s indices allocated once outside, or use SIMD-friendly structure.  
- Could use struct-of-arrays for `a,b,c,d,e` to allow vectorization, but here `a` is common output.  

Since vectorization failed in the compute loops, there is **optimization opportunity** → FAIL.  

### 4. Conclusion  
The optimized code preserves semantics but fails to vectorize the main computation loops due to indirect indexing, and has unnecessary malloc/free overhead.  

**Output**: FAIL

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.

    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

void s442_opt(int iterations,int LEN_1D, float* a, float *b,float* c, float *d, float* e, int* indx)
{
    // Pre-allocate index arrays once
    int* idx1 = (int*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(int));
    int* idx2 = (int*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(int));
    int* idx3 = (int*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(int));
    int* idx4 = (int*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(int));
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations/2; nl++) {
        // Count and collect indices in one pass
        int cnt1 = 0, cnt2 = 0, cnt3 = 0, cnt4 = 0;
        
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            int idx = indx[i];
            if (idx == 1) idx1[cnt1++] = i;
            else if (idx == 2) idx2[cnt2++] = i;
            else if (idx == 3) idx3[cnt3++] = i;
            else if (idx == 4) idx4[cnt4++] = i;
        }
        
        // Process each case with direct loops (no indirect indexing)
        // Use temporary arrays to store squared values first
        float* temp_b = (float*)malloc(cnt1 * sizeof(float));
        float* temp_c = (float*)malloc(cnt2 * sizeof(float));
        float* temp_d = (float*)malloc(cnt3 * sizeof(float));
        float* temp_e = (float*)malloc(cnt4 * sizeof(float));
        
        // Compute squared values (vectorizable)
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt1; j++) {
            int i = idx1[j];
            temp_b[j] = b[i] * b[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt2; j++) {
            int i = idx2[j];
            temp_c[j] = c[i] * c[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt3; j++) {
            int i = idx3[j];
            temp_d[j] = d[i] * d[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt4; j++) {
            int i = idx4[j];
            temp_e[j] = e[i] * e[i];
        }
        
        // Accumulate to a (vectorizable)
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt1; j++) {
            int i = idx1[j];
            a[i] += temp_b[j];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt2; j++) {
            int i = idx2[j];
            a[i] += temp_c[j];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt3; j++) {
            int i = idx3[j];
            a[i] += temp_d[j];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt4; j++) {
            int i = idx4[j];
            a[i] += temp_e[j];
        }
        
        free(temp_b);
        free(temp_c);
        free(temp_d);
        free(temp_e);
    }
    
    free(idx1);
    free(idx2);
    free(idx3);
    free(idx4);
}

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
Let’s go step by step.

---

## **1. Compilation errors in optimized code**

The optimized code compiles (no syntax errors), but the compiler gives remarks about vectorization issues, not compilation failures.  
So we can proceed to semantic comparison.

---

## **2. Semantic comparison with simple test cases**

The original code processes each element `i` in order, and for each `i`, based on `indx[i]`, it adds the square of one of `b[i]`, `c[i]`, `d[i]`, or `e[i]` to `a[i]`.  
The optimized code tries to group indices by case and process them in separate loops.

**Potential issue**: The original code runs `iterations/2` times, and each iteration uses the **same** `indx` array but `a[i]` accumulates across iterations.  
The optimized code recomputes the index grouping **inside** the `nl` loop each time, which is correct because `indx` doesn’t change. However, the order of accumulation into `a[i]` is different: original does all cases interleaved in the original `i` order; optimized does case 1 indices first, then case 2, etc.  

Since accumulation into `a[i]` is commutative (`a[i] += ...`), order doesn’t matter for final `a[i]` values **if** each `a[i]` is only updated once per outer iteration.  
But wait — in the original, each `i` is visited once per outer iteration, and exactly one of the four square terms is added. In the optimized version, each `i` is also visited exactly once per outer iteration (because `indx[i]` is fixed and each `i` is placed into exactly one of the four index arrays). So the set of updates is the same.

Let’s test with a tiny example:

**Example 1**:  
`LEN_1D = 3`, `iterations = 2` (so `nl` runs once)  
`indx = [1, 2, 1]`  
`a = [0, 0, 0]`, `b = [1, 0, 2]`, `c = [0, 3, 0]`, `d = e = zeros`.

Original:  
i=0: case 1 → a[0] += b[0]² = 1  
i=1: case 2 → a[1] += c[1]² = 9  
i=2: case 1 → a[2] += b[2]² = 4  
Result: a = [1, 9, 4]

Optimized:  
First pass: cnt1=2, idx1=[0,2]; cnt2=1, idx2=[1]; cnt3=0; cnt4=0.  
Compute temp_b[0]=1, temp_b[1]=4; temp_c[0]=9.  
Accumulate: a[0]+=1, a[2]+=4, a[1]+=9 → a = [1, 9, 4] ✅ same.

**Example 2**:  
`LEN_1D = 4`, `indx = [3, 3, 4, 2]`, `a = [10, 20, 30, 40]`, `d = [1, 2, 0, 0]`, `e = [0, 0, 5, 0]`, `c = [0, 0, 0, 6]`, `b = zeros`.

Original:  
i=0: case 3 → a[0] += 1 → 11  
i=1: case 3 → a[1] += 4 → 24  
i=2: case 4 → a[2] += 25 → 55  
i=3: case 2 → a[3] += 36 → 76  
Result: [11, 24, 55, 76]

Optimized:  
cnt3=2 idx3=[0,1], cnt4=1 idx4=[2], cnt2=1 idx2=[3]  
temp_d = [1, 4], temp_e=[25], temp_c=[36]  
Accumulate in order:  
a[0]+=1 → 11, a[1]+=4 → 24, a[2]+=25 → 55, a[3]+=36 → 76 ✅ same.

**Example 3** (multiple iterations):  
`iterations=4` → `nl` runs twice.  
`LEN_1D=2`, `indx=[1,4]`, `a=[0,0]`, `b=[2,0]`, `e=[0,3]`.

Original:  
nl=0: i=0: a[0]+=4 → 4; i=1: a[1]+=9 → 9  
nl=1: i=0: a[0]+=4 → 8; i=1: a[1]+=9 → 18  
Result: [8, 18]

Optimized:  
Each nl: cnt1=1 idx1=[0], cnt4=1 idx4=[1]  
First nl: a[0]+=4, a[1]+=9 → [4,9]  
Second nl: a[0]+=4, a[1]+=9 → [8,18] ✅ same.

So **semantics are preserved** despite reordering, because each `a[i]` update is independent and order within an iteration doesn’t matter.

---

## **3. Vectorization analysis**

Compiler feedback says:

- The first loop over `i` (line 19) not vectorized because it contains a switch (transformed to if-else chain) and `cnt1`, `cnt2`, etc., are used outside (reduction-like but not recognized).
- The square-computation loops (lines 35, 39, 43, 47) are interleaved by 4 but deemed “not beneficial” by cost model.
- The accumulation loops (lines 55, 59, 63, 67) not vectorized due to “unsafe indirect dependence” — because `a[i]` could alias with `temp_b`, etc. (though they don’t in practice, compiler can’t prove it).

**Why not vectorized fully?**  
The main issue: indirect indexing (`a[idx1[j]]`) in accumulation loops prevents vectorization due to possible aliasing and non-contiguous access.  
The square-computation loops could be vectorized (and are interleaved), but the cost model thinks it’s not beneficial due to small counts maybe.

**Optimization opportunity**:  
We can improve by removing the temporary arrays `temp_b`, etc., and fusing the square and accumulate into one loop per case, which also removes the aliasing concern for vectorizer.

Example rewrite for case 1:

```c
for (int j = 0; j < cnt1; j++) {
    int i = idx1[j];
    a[i] += b[i] * b[i];
}
```

Now no temporary array, so no false aliasing issue, and loop is vectorizable if `a`, `b` are not aliased with `idx1`.  
But `a[i]` and `b[i]` are accessed with indirect index `i`, still non-contiguous memory access, which may prevent SIMD vectorization on some architectures, but at least no compiler-reported aliasing issue.

Given the feedback, the current optimized code is **not fully vectorized** (accumulation loops not vectorized), so there is optimization opportunity → **FAIL**.

---

## **4. Conclusion**

The optimized code preserves semantics but fails to achieve full vectorization due to indirect access aliasing concerns and temporary arrays.  
It can be improved (e.g., fuse square and accumulate, remove temporary arrays), so it’s **not fully optimized**.

**Final decision**: FAIL

        // Count and collect indices in one pass
        int cnt1 = 0, cnt2 = 0, cnt3 = 0, cnt4 = 0;
        
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            int idx = indx[i];
            if (idx == 1) idx1[cnt1++] = i;
            else if (idx == 2) idx2[cnt2++] = i;
            else if (idx == 3) idx3[cnt3++] = i;
            else if (idx == 4) idx4[cnt4++] = i;
        }
        
        // Process each case with direct loops (no indirect indexing)
        // Use temporary arrays to store squared values first
        float* temp_b = (float*)malloc(cnt1 * sizeof(float));
        float* temp_c = (float*)malloc(cnt2 * sizeof(float));
        float* temp_d = (float*)malloc(cnt3 * sizeof(float));
        float* temp_e = (float*)malloc(cnt4 * sizeof(float));
        
        // Compute squared values (vectorizable)
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt1; j++) {
            int i = idx1[j];
            temp_b[j] = b[i] * b[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt2; j++) {
            int i = idx2[j];
            temp_c[j] = c[i] * c[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt3; j++) {
            int i = idx3[j];
            temp_d[j] = d[i] * d[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt4; j++) {
            int i = idx4[j];
            temp_e[j] = e[i] * e[i];
        }
        
        // Accumulate to a (vectorizable)
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt1; j++) {
            int i = idx1[j];
            a[i] += temp_b[j];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt2; j++) {
            int i = idx2[j];
            a[i] += temp_c[j];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt3; j++) {
            int i = idx3[j];
            a[i] += temp_d[j];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt4; j++) {
            int i = idx4[j];
            a[i] += temp_e[j];
        }
        
        free(temp_b);
        free(temp_c);
        free(temp_d);
        free(temp_e);
    }
    
    free(idx1);
    free(idx2);
    free(idx3);
    free(idx4);
}

Feedback:
Let’s go step by step.

---

## **1. Compilation errors in optimized code**

The optimized code compiles (no syntax errors), but the compiler gives remarks about vectorization issues, not compilation failures.  
So we can proceed to semantic comparison.

---

## **2. Semantic comparison with simple test cases**

The original code processes each element `i` in order, and for each `i`, based on `indx[i]`, it adds the square of one of `b[i]`, `c[i]`, `d[i]`, or `e[i]` to `a[i]`.  
The optimized code tries to group indices by case and process them in separate loops.

**Potential issue**: The original code runs `iterations/2` times, and each iteration uses the **same** `indx` array but `a[i]` accumulates across iterations.  
The optimized code recomputes the index grouping **inside** the `nl` loop each time, which is correct because `indx` doesn’t change. However, the order of accumulation into `a[i]` is different: original does all cases interleaved in the original `i` order; optimized does case 1 indices first, then case 2, etc.  

Since accumulation into `a[i]` is commutative (`a[i] += ...`), order doesn’t matter for final `a[i]` values **if** each `a[i]` is only updated once per outer iteration.  
But wait — in the original, each `i` is visited once per outer iteration, and exactly one of the four square terms is added. In the optimized version, each `i` is also visited exactly once per outer iteration (because `indx[i]` is fixed and each `i` is placed into exactly one of the four index arrays). So the set of updates is the same.

Let’s test with a tiny example:

**Example 1**:  
`LEN_1D = 3`, `iterations = 2` (so `nl` runs once)  
`indx = [1, 2, 1]`  
`a = [0, 0, 0]`, `b = [1, 0, 2]`, `c = [0, 3, 0]`, `d = e = zeros`.

Original:  
i=0: case 1 → a[0] += b[0]² = 1  
i=1: case 2 → a[1] += c[1]² = 9  
i=2: case 1 → a[2] += b[2]² = 4  
Result: a = [1, 9, 4]

Optimized:  
First pass: cnt1=2, idx1=[0,2]; cnt2=1, idx2=[1]; cnt3=0; cnt4=0.  
Compute temp_b[0]=1, temp_b[1]=4; temp_c[0]=9.  
Accumulate: a[0]+=1, a[2]+=4, a[1]+=9 → a = [1, 9, 4] ✅ same.

**Example 2**:  
`LEN_1D = 4`, `indx = [3, 3, 4, 2]`, `a = [10, 20, 30, 40]`, `d = [1, 2, 0, 0]`, `e = [0, 0, 5, 0]`, `c = [0, 0, 0, 6]`, `b = zeros`.

Original:  
i=0: case 3 → a[0] += 1 → 11  
i=1: case 3 → a[1] += 4 → 24  
i=2: case 4 → a[2] += 25 → 55  
i=3: case 2 → a[3] += 36 → 76  
Result: [11, 24, 55, 76]

Optimized:  
cnt3=2 idx3=[0,1], cnt4=1 idx4=[2], cnt2=1 idx2=[3]  
temp_d = [1, 4], temp_e=[25], temp_c=[36]  
Accumulate in order:  
a[0]+=1 → 11, a[1]+=4 → 24, a[2]+=25 → 55, a[3]+=36 → 76 ✅ same.

**Example 3** (multiple iterations):  
`iterations=4` → `nl` runs twice.  
`LEN_1D=2`, `indx=[1,4]`, `a=[0,0]`, `b=[2,0]`, `e=[0,3]`.

Original:  
nl=0: i=0: a[0]+=4 → 4; i=1: a[1]+=9 → 9  
nl=1: i=0: a[0]+=4 → 8; i=1: a[1]+=9 → 18  
Result: [8, 18]

Optimized:  
Each nl: cnt1=1 idx1=[0], cnt4=1 idx4=[1]  
First nl: a[0]+=4, a[1]+=9 → [4,9]  
Second nl: a[0]+=4, a[1]+=9 → [8,18] ✅ same.

So **semantics are preserved** despite reordering, because each `a[i]` update is independent and order within an iteration doesn’t matter.

---

## **3. Vectorization analysis**

Compiler feedback says:

- The first loop over `i` (line 19) not vectorized because it contains a switch (transformed to if-else chain) and `cnt1`, `cnt2`, etc., are used outside (reduction-like but not recognized).
- The square-computation loops (lines 35, 39, 43, 47) are interleaved by 4 but deemed “not beneficial” by cost model.
- The accumulation loops (lines 55, 59, 63, 67) not vectorized due to “unsafe indirect dependence” — because `a[i]` could alias with `temp_b`, etc. (though they don’t in practice, compiler can’t prove it).

**Why not vectorized fully?**  
The main issue: indirect indexing (`a[idx1[j]]`) in accumulation loops prevents vectorization due to possible aliasing and non-contiguous access.  
The square-computation loops could be vectorized (and are interleaved), but the cost model thinks it’s not beneficial due to small counts maybe.

**Optimization opportunity**:  
We can improve by removing the temporary arrays `temp_b`, etc., and fusing the square and accumulate into one loop per case, which also removes the aliasing concern for vectorizer.

Example rewrite for case 1:

```c
for (int j = 0; j < cnt1; j++) {
    int i = idx1[j];
    a[i] += b[i] * b[i];
}
```

Now no temporary array, so no false aliasing issue, and loop is vectorizable if `a`, `b` are not aliased with `idx1`.  
But `a[i]` and `b[i]` are accessed with indirect index `i`, still non-contiguous memory access, which may prevent SIMD vectorization on some architectures, but at least no compiler-reported aliasing issue.

Given the feedback, the current optimized code is **not fully vectorized** (accumulation loops not vectorized), so there is optimization opportunity → **FAIL**.

---

## **4. Conclusion**

The optimized code preserves semantics but fails to achieve full vectorization due to indirect access aliasing concerns and temporary arrays.  
It can be improved (e.g., fuse square and accumulate, remove temporary arrays), so it’s **not fully optimized**.

**Final decision**: FAIL

Unit Test analysis: 
Source code and optimized code semantics are inconsistent.


**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

## Analysis

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code
The optimized code compiles successfully (no compilation errors reported). The compiler feedback shows only optimization remarks, not errors.

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases

**Test Case 1: Basic functionality**
- Source: LEN_1D=4, iterations=2, indx=[1,2,3,4]
- Original: Processes each element twice (iterations/2=1), adds squared values based on indx
- Optimized: Same behavior - collects indices once per outer loop iteration, processes each case

**Test Case 2: Mixed indices with repeats**
- Source: LEN_1D=5, indx=[1,1,2,2,3]
- Original: Processes sequentially, switching based on indx[i]
- Optimized: Groups by index value, processes all elements with same index together
- **Semantic difference**: The optimized code processes elements in different order within each iteration. However, since each `a[i]` update is independent (no dependencies between different i values), the final result is identical.

**Test Case 3: Multiple outer loop iterations**
- Source: iterations=4 (so nl loops twice)
- Original: Processes all elements twice in original order
- Optimized: Re-groups indices in each outer iteration, processes grouped
- Result: Still identical since operations are independent per element

**Conclusion**: The semantics are preserved. The optimized code correctly computes the same results as the original because:
- Each `a[i]` update depends only on `a[i]` and one other array element
- No cross-element dependencies exist
- The grouping by index doesn't change the mathematical result

### 3. Vectorization Analysis

**Compiler Feedback Analysis**:
The compiler reports several issues preventing vectorization:

1. **Index collection loop (lines 19-26)**: Contains a switch-like if-else chain and writes to multiple arrays with variable indices (`cnt1++`, `cnt2++`, etc.). This cannot be vectorized due to:
   - Data-dependent control flow
   - Scatter operations with variable indices
   - Multiple accumulating counters

void s442_opt(int iterations,int LEN_1D, float* a, float *b,float* c, float *d, float* e, int* indx)
{
    // Pre-allocate index arrays once
    int* idx1 = (int*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(int));
    int* idx2 = (int*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(int));
    int* idx3 = (int*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(int));
    int* idx4 = (int*)malloc(LEN_1D * sizeof(int));
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations/2; nl++) {
        // Count and collect indices in one pass
        int cnt1 = 0, cnt2 = 0, cnt3 = 0, cnt4 = 0;
        
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            int idx = indx[i];
            if (idx == 1) idx1[cnt1++] = i;
            else if (idx == 2) idx2[cnt2++] = i;
            else if (idx == 3) idx3[cnt3++] = i;
            else if (idx == 4) idx4[cnt4++] = i;
        }
        
        // Process each case with fused square and accumulate (no temporary arrays)
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt1; j++) {
            int i = idx1[j];
            a[i] += b[i] * b[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt2; j++) {
            int i = idx2[j];
            a[i] += c[i] * c[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt3; j++) {
            int i = idx3[j];
            a[i] += d[i] * d[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt4; j++) {
            int i = idx4[j];
            a[i] += e[i] * e[i];
        }
    }
    
    free(idx1);
    free(idx2);
    free(idx3);
    free(idx4);
}

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

Let’s go step-by-step.  

---

**1. Compilation errors in optimized code**  
The optimized code compiles without errors — no syntax issues.  

---

**2. Semantic comparison**  

**Source code behavior:**  
- For each `i`, `indx[i]` determines which of `b[i]`, `c[i]`, `d[i]`, or `e[i]` is squared and added to `a[i]`.  
- Only **one** of the four possible updates is applied per iteration of the inner loop.  
- The outer loop runs `iterations/2` times, each time performing the same switch-based update for all `i`.  

**Optimized code behavior:**  
- It splits the inner loop into four separate loops over all `i`, each checking `indx[i]` for a different case.  
- In the **first pass** (first inner loop), if `indx[i]==1`, it does `a[i] += b[i]*b[i]`.  
- In the **second pass** (second inner loop), if `indx[i]==2`, it does `a[i] += c[i]*c[i]`.  
- This means:  
  - If `indx[i]==1`, it gets updated in pass 1, but in pass 2, `indx[i]` is still 1, so it doesn’t match case 2 — so no extra update.  
  - However, **the order of updates across cases is different** from the original? Wait — in the original, for a given `i`, only one case matches, so only one update per outer loop iteration.  
  - In the optimized version, for a given `i`, only one of the four inner loops will match its `indx[i]`, so only one update per outer loop iteration.  

But there’s a **critical difference**:  
In the original, within a single iteration of the inner loop over `i`, each `i` is processed exactly once, updating `a[i]` based on its `indx[i]`.  
In the optimized version, the four inner loops are executed sequentially, but each still loops over all `i`.  

Let’s test with a simple example:  
- `LEN_1D = 2`, `iterations = 2` → outer loop runs once (`iterations/2 = 1`).  
- `indx[0] = 1`, `indx[1] = 2`.  
- Initial `a[0] = a[1] = 0`, `b[0] = 1`, `c[1] = 2`.  

**Original:**  
Outer loop once → inner loop `i=0`: case 1 → `a[0] += 1*1 = 1`.  
Inner loop `i=1`: case 2 → `a[1] += 2*2 = 4`.  
Result: `a[0]=1`, `a[1]=4`.  

**Optimized:**  
Outer loop once →  
First inner loop (`indx[i]==1`): `i=0` matches → `a[0] = 1`. `i=1` no match.  
Second inner loop (`indx[i]==2`): `i=0` no match, `i=1` matches → `a[1] = 4`.  
Third inner loop: no matches.  
Fourth inner loop: no matches.  
Result: `a[0]=1`, `a[1]=4`.  

Matches.  

But what if `indx[i]` changes between passes? It doesn’t — `indx` is constant.  
What if `a[i]` is updated in multiple passes for same `i`? Not possible because `indx[i]` is fixed, so only one of the four conditions will be true.  

So semantically, they are **identical** for fixed `indx`.

**3. Vectorization analysis**  

The optimized code still has **conditionals inside each loop** (`if (indx[i] == k)`).  
This means the compiler must generate masked operations or scalar code.  
But now each loop’s condition is uniform per case, which might allow **autovectorization with gather/scatter or masked stores** if the architecture supports it (AVX-512).  

However, the compiler feedback says “UNIT TEST PASS” — meaning the unit test passed, but we don’t have compiler vectorization report here.  
Given the structure:  

- Original: switch with goto → hard to vectorize due to control flow divergence.  
- Optimized: split into separate loops, each with a simple `if` — easier to vectorize with masking.  

But the loops are still not trivially vectorizable without masking support, because the store to `a[i]` is conditional.  
If the compiler sees `indx` as invariant in the inner loops, it could potentially vectorize with masked operations.  

**Potential improvement**:  
We could gather indices for each case before the loop, then run a purely data-parallel loop without conditionals.

This removes conditionals entirely and enables straightforward vectorization.  

Since the current optimized code still relies on conditionals inside the loop, it may not be fully vectorized on targets without predication.  
Thus, there is **optimization opportunity** (removing conditionals via index gathering).

In the original, within a single iteration of the inner loop over `i`, each `i` is processed exactly once, updating `a[i]` based on its `indx[i]`.  
In the optimized version, the four inner loops are executed sequentially, but each still loops over all `i`.

Inner loop `i=1`: case 2 → `a[1] += 2*2 = 4`.

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

void s442_opt(int iterations,int LEN_1D, float* a, float *b,float* c, float *d, float* e, int* indx)
{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations/2; nl++) {
        // Gather indices for each case to eliminate conditionals
        int idx1[LEN_1D], idx2[LEN_1D], idx3[LEN_1D], idx4[LEN_1D];
        int cnt1 = 0, cnt2 = 0, cnt3 = 0, cnt4 = 0;
        
        // Single pass to categorize indices
        for (int i = 0; i < LEN_1D; i++) {
            switch (indx[i]) {
                case 1: idx1[cnt1++] = i; break;
                case 2: idx2[cnt2++] = i; break;
                case 3: idx3[cnt3++] = i; break;
                case 4: idx4[cnt4++] = i; break;
            }
        }
        
        // Process each case without conditionals
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt1; j++) {
            int i = idx1[j];
            a[i] += b[i] * b[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt2; j++) {
            int i = idx2[j];
            a[i] += c[i] * c[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt3; j++) {
            int i = idx3[j];
            a[i] += d[i] * d[i];
        }
        for (int j = 0; j < cnt4; j++) {
            int i = idx4[j];
            a[i] += e[i] * e[i];
        }
    }
}

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
## Analysis

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code
The optimized code compiles successfully but has a critical issue: it uses variable-length arrays (VLAs) with size `LEN_1D`, which may cause stack overflow if `LEN_1D` is large. While this is not a compilation error in C99, it's a potential runtime issue. However, since the compiler didn't report compilation errors, we proceed.

### 2. Semantic Comparison
The optimized code attempts to eliminate conditionals by gathering indices into separate arrays for each case. However, there's a **semantic difference**:

**Source Code Behavior:**
- For each iteration of `nl`, the switch statement is evaluated for every `i`
- The same `indx[i]` value is used repeatedly across different `nl` iterations
- The computation `a[i] += ...` accumulates results across iterations

**Optimized Code Behavior:**
- The index categorization happens **inside** the `nl` loop
- This means for each `nl` iteration, indices are re-categorized
- The accumulation still happens correctly

However, there's a more subtle issue: the optimized code processes all indices of case 1 first, then case 2, etc. This changes the **order of accumulation** compared to the original sequential processing. Since floating-point addition is not associative, this could lead to different numerical results due to rounding differences.

Let's test with a simple example:
- `LEN_1D = 3`, `iterations = 2`
- `indx = [1, 2, 1]`
- `a = [0, 0, 0]`, `b = [1, 0, 2]`, `c = [0, 1, 0]`

**Original code execution:**
- `nl = 0`: i=0: a[0] += 1*1 = 1; i=1: a[1] += 1*1 = 1; i=2: a[2] += 4 = 4
- `nl = 1`: i=0: a[0] += 1 = 2; i=1: a[1] += 1 = 2; i=2: a[2] += 4 = 8

**Optimized code execution:**
- `nl = 0`: idx1 = [0, 2], idx2 = [1]
  - Process idx1: a[0] += 1 = 1; a[2] += 4 = 4
  - Process idx2: a[1] += 1 = 1
- `nl = 1`: idx1 = [0, 2], idx2 = [1]
  - Process idx1: a[0] += 1 = 2; a[2] += 4 = 8
  - Process idx2: a[1] += 1 = 2

The results match numerically in this case, but floating-point non-associativity could cause differences in other cases.

More critically, the optimized code has **performance issues**:
1. It allocates 4 arrays of size `LEN_1D` for each iteration
2. It performs extra passes through the data

### 3. Vectorization Analysis
The compiler feedback shows:
- The original loop contains a switch statement preventing vectorization
- The optimized loops have bounds that cannot be identified by the compiler

The main issue with vectorization is that the computation depends on `indx[i]`, creating data-dependent control flow. The optimized code attempts to solve this by gathering indices, but the compiler still cannot determine array bounds for the gathered index arrays.

### 4. Optimization Opportunities
The current optimization has several problems:
1. **Stack overflow risk** with large `LEN_1D`
2. **Extra memory allocation** inside the hot loop
3. **Multiple passes** through the data
4. **Still not vectorizable** according to compiler

A better approach would be to use **loop splitting with predication** or **manual vectorization with gather operations**.

### Conclusion
The optimized code has **semantic issues** due to floating-point non-associativity from changed computation order and has **performance issues** from excessive memory allocation. The compiler also indicates it's **not vectorizable**.

**OUTPUT: FAIL**

The optimized code needs significant modifications to be both semantically correct and vectorization-friendly.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:

    else

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
Let’s go step-by-step.  

---

**1. Compilation errors in the optimized code**  
The optimized code compiles without errors in a C compiler (assuming C99 or later).  
No syntax errors are present.  

---

**2. Semantic comparison with simple test cases**  

The original code uses a `switch` with `goto` labels, meaning for each `i`, exactly one of the four cases is executed, and `a[i]` is incremented by the square of one of `b[i]`, `c[i]`, `d[i]`, or `e[i]`.  

The optimized code uses:  
```c
temp += (idx == 1) * (b[i] * b[i]);
temp += (idx == 2) * (c[i] * c[i]);
...
a[i] += temp;
```
In C, `(idx == k)` evaluates to `1` if true, `0` if false.  
If `idx` is between 1 and 4, exactly one of the four conditions is true, so `temp` gets exactly one square term — same as original.  
If `idx` is not 1–4, all conditions false, `temp = 0`, `a[i]` unchanged — same as original (which would skip all cases and go to `L50` doing nothing).  

So **semantics are identical**.  

But the unit test analysis says they are inconsistent — this suggests maybe the test used `indx[i]` values outside 1–4, or maybe floating-point multiplication by `(idx == k)` being integer `1` is fine, but if `b[i]` is NaN/Inf, multiplication by `0` or `1` could behave differently? Actually, multiplying by `0` yields `0.0` (if `0` is integer promoted to `float`), but in original, that term is never computed.  

Wait — in original, only one of `b[i]*b[i]`, `c[i]*c[i]`, etc., is computed.  
In optimized, **all four are computed** before selecting via multiplication by 0/1.  
If any of `b[i]`, `c[i]`, `d[i]`, `e[i]` is NaN, computing `NaN * NaN` gives NaN, and `0 * NaN` is NaN in IEEE 754.  
Thus, if `idx == 1`, we compute:  
```
temp = 1*(b[i]*b[i]) + 0*NaN + 0*NaN + 0*NaN
```
But `0*NaN` is NaN, so `temp = b[i]*b[i] + NaN + NaN + NaN = NaN`.  
So the result becomes NaN even though only `b[i]*b[i]` should contribute.  

That’s the bug: **side effects of computing all squares** when some inputs may be NaN (or signaling NaN could raise exceptions).  

So semantics differ in floating-point exceptional cases.  

---

**Example**:  
Let `b[i]=1.0`, `c[i]=NaN`, `d[i]=2.0`, `e[i]=3.0`, `indx[i]=1`.  
Original: `a[i] += 1.0*1.0 = 1.0`  
Optimized:  
`temp = 1*(1.0*1.0) + 0*(NaN*NaN) + 0*(4.0) + 0*(9.0)`  
But `0*(NaN*NaN)` is `0*NaN = NaN`, so `temp = 1.0 + NaN + 0.0 + 0.0 = NaN`.  
`a[i] += NaN → NaN`.  

Thus **FAIL** due to semantic difference.  

---

**3. Vectorization analysis**  
Not needed because semantics are wrong.  

---

**Conclusion**:  
The optimized code changes the order of evaluation and introduces arithmetic with NaN in cases that were not executed in the original, breaking floating-point semantics.  

**Output**: FAIL

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

void s442_opt(int iterations,int LEN_1D, float* a, float *b,float* c, float *d, float* e, int* indx)
{
    for (int nl = 0; nl < iterations/2; nl++) {
        // Gather indices for each case

                case 2: idx2[cnt2++] = i; break;
                case 3: idx3[cnt3++] = i; break;
                case 4: idx4[cnt4++] = i; break;

        }
    }
}

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
Let’s go step by step.

---

### **1. Compilation errors in optimized code**
The optimized code compiles without errors (confirmed by the compiler feedback showing vectorization remarks).

---

### **2. Semantic comparison with simple test cases**

**Source code behavior:**
- For each `i`, exactly one `case` is executed based on `indx[i]`.
- Only one of `b[i]*b[i]`, `c[i]*c[i]`, `d[i]*d[i]`, or `e[i]*e[i]` is added to `a[i]` per iteration of the inner loop.

**Optimized code behavior:**
- It runs **four separate loops** per outer iteration.
- In each loop, it adds a term to `a[i]` **only if** `indx[i]` matches that case.
- However, if `indx[i]` is not 1, `cond1` is 0, so `a[i]` gets `a[i] + 0` in the first loop, etc.
- This means **each `a[i]` is visited four times** per outer iteration, but only one of those visits adds a nonzero value (the one matching `indx[i]`), the others add 0.

**Key difference:**
- In the original code, each `i` is processed **once** per outer iteration, with a single addition.
- In the optimized code, each `i` is processed **four times** per outer iteration, with three useless adds of 0 and one real addition.

Mathematically, the result is the same because adding 0 doesn’t change the value.  
But there’s a **critical hidden issue**:  
If `a[i]` is a floating-point number, and we do four separate additions instead of one, we introduce **extra rounding steps** (each addition has a rounding error).  
Thus, after many outer iterations (`iterations/2` times), the floating-point results can differ due to accumulated rounding differences.  
So **semantics are not bitwise identical** in floating-point arithmetic.

**Example to illustrate:**
Let `iterations = 2`, `LEN_1D = 1`, `a[0] = 1.0`, `b[0] = 1e-8`, `indx[0] = 1`, others irrelevant.

Original:

**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```
